Enter Lady Capulet.




LADY CAPULET.

What noise is here?





NURSE.

O lamentable day!





LADY CAPULET.

What is the matter?





NURSE.

Look, look! O heavy day!





LADY CAPULET.

O me, O me! My child, my only life.

Revive, look up, or I will die with thee.

Help, help! Call help.




Enter Capulet.




CAPULET.

For shame, bring Juliet forth, her lord is come.





NURSE.

She’s dead, deceas’d, she’s dead; alack the day!





LADY CAPULET.

Alack the day, she’s dead, she’s dead, she’s dead!





CAPULET.

Ha! Let me see her. Out alas! She’s cold,

Her blood is settled and her joints are stiff.

Life and these lips have long been separated.

Death lies on her like an untimely frost

Upon the sweetest flower of all the field.





NURSE.

O lamentable day!





LADY CAPULET.

O woful time!





CAPULET.

Death, that hath ta’en her hence to make me wail,

Ties up my tongue and will not let me speak.




Enter Friar Lawrence and
Paris with Musicians.




FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Come, is the bride ready to go to church?





CAPULET.

Ready to go, but never to return.

O son, the night before thy wedding day

Hath death lain with thy bride. There she lies,

Flower as she was, deflowered by him.

Death is my son-in-law, death is my heir;

My daughter he hath wedded. I will die

And leave him all; life, living, all is death’s.





PARIS.

Have I thought long to see this morning’s face,

And doth it give me such a sight as this?





LADY CAPULET.

Accurs’d, unhappy, wretched, hateful day.

Most miserable hour that e’er time saw

In lasting labour of his pilgrimage.

But one, poor one, one poor and loving child,

But one thing to rejoice and solace in,

And cruel death hath catch’d it from my sight.





NURSE.

O woe! O woeful, woeful, woeful day.

Most lamentable day, most woeful day

That ever, ever, I did yet behold!

O day, O day, O day, O hateful day.

Never was seen so black a day as this.

O woeful day, O woeful day.





PARIS.

Beguil’d, divorced, wronged, spited, slain.

Most detestable death, by thee beguil’d,

By cruel, cruel thee quite overthrown.

O love! O life! Not life, but love in death!





CAPULET.

Despis’d, distressed, hated, martyr’d, kill’d.

Uncomfortable time, why cam’st thou now

To murder, murder our solemnity?

O child! O child! My soul, and not my child,

Dead art thou. Alack, my child is dead,

And with my child my joys are buried.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Peace, ho, for shame. Confusion’s cure lives not

In these confusions. Heaven and yourself

Had part in this fair maid, now heaven hath all,

And all the better is it for the maid.

Your part in her you could not keep from death,

But heaven keeps his part in eternal life.

The most you sought was her promotion,

For ’twas your heaven she should be advanc’d,

And weep ye now, seeing she is advanc’d

Above the clouds, as high as heaven itself?

O, in this love, you love your child so ill

That you run mad, seeing that she is well.

She’s not well married that lives married long,

But she’s best married that dies married young.

Dry up your tears, and stick your rosemary

On this fair corse, and, as the custom is,

And in her best array bear her to church;

For though fond nature bids us all lament,

Yet nature’s tears are reason’s merriment.





CAPULET.

All things that we ordained festival

Turn from their office to black funeral:

Our instruments to melancholy bells,

Our wedding cheer to a sad burial feast;

Our solemn hymns to sullen dirges change;

Our bridal flowers serve for a buried corse,

And all things change them to the contrary.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Sir, go you in, and, madam, go with him,

And go, Sir Paris, everyone prepare

To follow this fair corse unto her grave.

The heavens do lower upon you for some ill;

Move them no more by crossing their high will.




[Exeunt Capulet, Lady Capulet, Paris
and Friar.]




FIRST MUSICIAN.

Faith, we may put up our pipes and be gone.




NURSE.

Honest good fellows, ah, put up, put up,

For well you know this is a pitiful case.





FIRST MUSICIAN.

Ay, by my troth, the case may be amended.



[Exit Nurse.]



Enter Peter.





PETER.

Musicians, O, musicians, ‘Heart’s ease,’
‘Heart’s ease’, O, and you will have me live, play
‘Heart’s ease.’





FIRST MUSICIAN.

Why ‘Heart’s ease’?





PETER.

O musicians, because my heart itself plays ‘My heart is
full’. O play me some merry dump to comfort me.





FIRST MUSICIAN.

Not a dump we, ’tis no time to play now.





PETER.

You will not then?





FIRST MUSICIAN.

No.




PETER.

I will then give it you soundly.





FIRST MUSICIAN.

What will you give us?





PETER.

No money, on my faith, but the gleek! I will give you the
minstrel.





FIRST MUSICIAN.

Then will I give you the serving-creature.





PETER.

Then will I lay the serving-creature’s dagger on your pate. I will carry
no crotchets. I’ll re you, I’ll fa you. Do you note me?





FIRST MUSICIAN.

And you re us and fa us, you note us.





SECOND MUSICIAN.

Pray you put up your dagger, and put out your wit.





PETER.

Then have at you with my wit. I will dry-beat you with an
iron wit, and put up my iron dagger. Answer me like men.

    ‘When griping griefs the heart doth wound,

      And doleful dumps the mind oppress,

    Then music with her silver sound’—

Why ‘silver sound’? Why ‘music with her silver sound’?
What say you, Simon Catling?





FIRST MUSICIAN.

Marry, sir, because silver hath a sweet sound.





PETER.

Prates. What say you, Hugh Rebeck?





SECOND MUSICIAN.

I say ‘silver sound’ because musicians sound for silver.





PETER.

Prates too! What say you, James Soundpost?





THIRD MUSICIAN.

Faith, I know not what to say.





PETER.

O, I cry you mercy, you are the singer. I will say for you. It is ‘music
with her silver sound’ because musicians have no gold for sounding.

      ‘Then music with her silver sound

      With speedy help doth lend redress.’




[Exit.]




FIRST MUSICIAN.

What a pestilent knave is this same!





SECOND MUSICIAN.

Hang him, Jack. Come, we’ll in here, tarry for the mourners, and stay
dinner.




[Exeunt.]








 ACT V



 SCENE I. Mantua. A Street.



Enter Romeo.




ROMEO.

If I may trust the flattering eye of sleep,

My dreams presage some joyful news at hand.

My bosom’s lord sits lightly in his throne;

And all this day an unaccustom’d spirit

Lifts me above the ground with cheerful thoughts.

I dreamt my lady came and found me dead,—

Strange dream, that gives a dead man leave to think!—

And breath’d such life with kisses in my lips,

That I reviv’d, and was an emperor.

Ah me, how sweet is love itself possess’d,

When but love’s shadows are so rich in joy.




Enter Balthasar.




News from Verona! How now, Balthasar?

Dost thou not bring me letters from the Friar?

How doth my lady? Is my father well?

How fares my Juliet? That I ask again;

For nothing can be ill if she be well.





BALTHASAR.

Then she is well, and nothing can be ill.

Her body sleeps in Capel’s monument,

And her immortal part with angels lives.

I saw her laid low in her kindred’s vault,

And presently took post to tell it you.

O pardon me for bringing these ill news,

Since you did leave it for my office, sir.





ROMEO.

Is it even so? Then I defy you, stars!

Thou know’st my lodging. Get me ink and paper,

And hire post-horses. I will hence tonight.





BALTHASAR.

I do beseech you sir, have patience.

Your looks are pale and wild, and do import

Some misadventure.





ROMEO.

Tush, thou art deceiv’d.

Leave me, and do the thing I bid thee do.

Hast thou no letters to me from the Friar?





BALTHASAR.

No, my good lord.





ROMEO.

No matter. Get thee gone,

And hire those horses. I’ll be with thee straight.




[Exit Balthasar.]




Well, Juliet, I will lie with thee tonight.

Let’s see for means. O mischief thou art swift

To enter in the thoughts of desperate men.

I do remember an apothecary,—

And hereabouts he dwells,—which late I noted

In tatter’d weeds, with overwhelming brows,

Culling of simples, meagre were his looks,

Sharp misery had worn him to the bones;

And in his needy shop a tortoise hung,

An alligator stuff’d, and other skins

Of ill-shaped fishes; and about his shelves

A beggarly account of empty boxes,

Green earthen pots, bladders, and musty seeds,

Remnants of packthread, and old cakes of roses

Were thinly scatter’d, to make up a show.

Noting this penury, to myself I said,

And if a man did need a poison now,

Whose sale is present death in Mantua,

Here lives a caitiff wretch would sell it him.

O, this same thought did but forerun my need,

And this same needy man must sell it me.

As I remember, this should be the house.

Being holiday, the beggar’s shop is shut.

What, ho! Apothecary!




Enter Apothecary.




APOTHECARY.

Who calls so loud?





ROMEO.

Come hither, man. I see that thou art poor.

Hold, there is forty ducats. Let me have

A dram of poison, such soon-speeding gear

As will disperse itself through all the veins,

That the life-weary taker may fall dead,

And that the trunk may be discharg’d of breath

As violently as hasty powder fir’d

Doth hurry from the fatal cannon’s womb.





APOTHECARY.

Such mortal drugs I have, but Mantua’s law

Is death to any he that utters them.





ROMEO.

Art thou so bare and full of wretchedness,

And fear’st to die? Famine is in thy cheeks,

Need and oppression starveth in thine eyes,

Contempt and beggary hangs upon thy back.

The world is not thy friend, nor the world’s law;

The world affords no law to make thee rich;

Then be not poor, but break it and take this.





APOTHECARY.

My poverty, but not my will consents.





ROMEO.

I pay thy poverty, and not thy will.





APOTHECARY.

Put this in any liquid thing you will

And drink it off; and, if you had the strength

Of twenty men, it would despatch you straight.





ROMEO.

There is thy gold, worse poison to men’s souls,

Doing more murder in this loathsome world

Than these poor compounds that thou mayst not sell.

I sell thee poison, thou hast sold me none.

Farewell, buy food, and get thyself in flesh.

Come, cordial and not poison, go with me

To Juliet’s grave, for there must I use thee.




[Exeunt.]



 SCENE II. Friar Lawrence’s Cell.



Enter Friar John.




FRIAR JOHN.

Holy Franciscan Friar! Brother, ho!




Enter Friar Lawrence.




FRIAR LAWRENCE.

This same should be the voice of Friar John.

Welcome from Mantua. What says Romeo?

Or, if his mind be writ, give me his letter.





FRIAR JOHN.

Going to find a barefoot brother out,

One of our order, to associate me,

Here in this city visiting the sick,

And finding him, the searchers of the town,

Suspecting that we both were in a house

Where the infectious pestilence did reign,

Seal’d up the doors, and would not let us forth,

So that my speed to Mantua there was stay’d.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Who bare my letter then to Romeo?





FRIAR JOHN.

I could not send it,—here it is again,—

Nor get a messenger to bring it thee,

So fearful were they of infection.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Unhappy fortune! By my brotherhood,

The letter was not nice, but full of charge,

Of dear import, and the neglecting it

May do much danger. Friar John, go hence,

Get me an iron crow and bring it straight

Unto my cell.





FRIAR JOHN.

Brother, I’ll go and bring it thee.




[Exit.]




FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Now must I to the monument alone.

Within this three hours will fair Juliet wake.

She will beshrew me much that Romeo

Hath had no notice of these accidents;

But I will write again to Mantua,

And keep her at my cell till Romeo come.

Poor living corse, clos’d in a dead man’s tomb.




[Exit.]



 SCENE III. A churchyard; in it a Monument belonging to the Capulets.



Enter Paris, and his
Page bearing flowers and a torch.




PARIS.

Give me thy torch, boy. Hence and stand aloof.

Yet put it out, for I would not be seen.

Under yond yew tree lay thee all along,

Holding thy ear close to the hollow ground;

So shall no foot upon the churchyard tread,

Being loose, unfirm, with digging up of graves,

But thou shalt hear it. Whistle then to me,

As signal that thou hear’st something approach.

Give me those flowers. Do as I bid thee, go.





PAGE.

[Aside.] I am almost afraid to stand alone

Here in the churchyard; yet I will adventure.




[Retires.]




PARIS.

Sweet flower, with flowers thy bridal bed I strew.

O woe, thy canopy is dust and stones,

Which with sweet water nightly I will dew,

Or wanting that, with tears distill’d by moans.

The obsequies that I for thee will keep,

Nightly shall be to strew thy grave and weep.




[The Page whistles.]




The boy gives warning something doth approach.

What cursed foot wanders this way tonight,

To cross my obsequies and true love’s rite?

What, with a torch! Muffle me, night, awhile.




[Retires.]



Enter Romeo and
Balthasar with a torch, mattock, &c.




ROMEO.

Give me that mattock and the wrenching iron.

Hold, take this letter; early in the morning

See thou deliver it to my lord and father.

Give me the light; upon thy life I charge thee,

Whate’er thou hear’st or seest, stand all aloof

And do not interrupt me in my course.

Why I descend into this bed of death

Is partly to behold my lady’s face,

But chiefly to take thence from her dead finger

A precious ring, a ring that I must use

In dear employment. Therefore hence, be gone.

But if thou jealous dost return to pry

In what I further shall intend to do,

By heaven I will tear thee joint by joint,

And strew this hungry churchyard with thy limbs.

The time and my intents are savage-wild;

More fierce and more inexorable far

Than empty tigers or the roaring sea.





BALTHASAR.

I will be gone, sir, and not trouble you.





ROMEO.

So shalt thou show me friendship. Take thou that.

Live, and be prosperous, and farewell, good fellow.





BALTHASAR.

For all this same, I’ll hide me hereabout.

His looks I fear, and his intents I doubt.




[Retires]




ROMEO.

Thou detestable maw, thou womb of death,

Gorg’d with the dearest morsel of the earth,

Thus I enforce thy rotten jaws to open,




[Breaking open the door of the monument.]




And in despite, I’ll cram thee with more food.




PARIS.

This is that banish’d haughty Montague

That murder’d my love’s cousin,—with which grief,

It is supposed, the fair creature died,—

And here is come to do some villainous shame

To the dead bodies. I will apprehend him.




[Advances.]




Stop thy unhallow’d toil, vile Montague.

Can vengeance be pursu’d further than death?

Condemned villain, I do apprehend thee.

Obey, and go with me, for thou must die.





ROMEO.

I must indeed; and therefore came I hither.

Good gentle youth, tempt not a desperate man.

Fly hence and leave me. Think upon these gone;

Let them affright thee. I beseech thee, youth,

Put not another sin upon my head

By urging me to fury. O be gone.

By heaven I love thee better than myself;

For I come hither arm’d against myself.

Stay not, be gone, live, and hereafter say,

A madman’s mercy bid thee run away.





PARIS.

I do defy thy conjuration,

And apprehend thee for a felon here.





ROMEO.

Wilt thou provoke me? Then have at thee, boy!




[They fight.]




PAGE.

O lord, they fight! I will go call the watch.




[Exit.]




PARIS.

O, I am slain! [Falls.] If thou be merciful,

Open the tomb, lay me with Juliet.




[Dies.]




ROMEO.

In faith, I will. Let me peruse this face.

Mercutio’s kinsman, noble County Paris!

What said my man, when my betossed soul

Did not attend him as we rode? I think

He told me Paris should have married Juliet.

Said he not so? Or did I dream it so?

Or am I mad, hearing him talk of Juliet,

To think it was so? O, give me thy hand,

One writ with me in sour misfortune’s book.

I’ll bury thee in a triumphant grave.

A grave? O no, a lantern, slaught’red youth,

For here lies Juliet, and her beauty makes

This vault a feasting presence full of light.

Death, lie thou there, by a dead man interr’d.




[Laying Paris in the monument.]




How oft when men are at the point of death

Have they been merry! Which their keepers call

A lightning before death. O, how may I

Call this a lightning? O my love, my wife,

Death that hath suck’d the honey of thy breath,

Hath had no power yet upon thy beauty.

Thou art not conquer’d. Beauty’s ensign yet

Is crimson in thy lips and in thy cheeks,

And death’s pale flag is not advanced there.

Tybalt, liest thou there in thy bloody sheet?

O, what more favour can I do to thee

Than with that hand that cut thy youth in twain

To sunder his that was thine enemy?

Forgive me, cousin. Ah, dear Juliet,

Why art thou yet so fair? Shall I believe

That unsubstantial death is amorous;

And that the lean abhorred monster keeps

Thee here in dark to be his paramour?

For fear of that I still will stay with thee,

And never from this palace of dim night

Depart again. Here, here will I remain

With worms that are thy chambermaids. O, here

Will I set up my everlasting rest;

And shake the yoke of inauspicious stars

From this world-wearied flesh. Eyes, look your last.

Arms, take your last embrace! And, lips, O you

The doors of breath, seal with a righteous kiss

A dateless bargain to engrossing death.

Come, bitter conduct, come, unsavoury guide.

Thou desperate pilot, now at once run on

The dashing rocks thy sea-sick weary bark.

Here’s to my love! [Drinks.] O true apothecary!

Thy drugs are quick. Thus with a kiss I die.




[Dies.]



Enter, at the other end of the Churchyard,
Friar Lawrence, with a lantern, crow, and spade.




FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Saint Francis be my speed. How oft tonight

Have my old feet stumbled at graves? Who’s there?

Who is it that consorts, so late, the dead?





BALTHASAR.

Here’s one, a friend, and one that knows you well.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Bliss be upon you. Tell me, good my friend,

What torch is yond that vainly lends his light

To grubs and eyeless skulls? As I discern,

It burneth in the Capels’ monument.





BALTHASAR.

It doth so, holy sir, and there’s my master,

One that you love.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Who is it?





BALTHASAR.

Romeo.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

How long hath he been there?





BALTHASAR.

Full half an hour.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Go with me to the vault.





BALTHASAR.

I dare not, sir;

My master knows not but I am gone hence,

And fearfully did menace me with death

If I did stay to look on his intents.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Stay then, I’ll go alone. Fear comes upon me.

O, much I fear some ill unlucky thing.





BALTHASAR.

As I did sleep under this yew tree here,

I dreamt my master and another fought,

And that my master slew him.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

Romeo! [Advances.]

Alack, alack, what blood is this which stains

The stony entrance of this sepulchre?

What mean these masterless and gory swords

To lie discolour’d by this place of peace?




[Enters the monument.]




Romeo! O, pale! Who else? What, Paris too?

And steep’d in blood? Ah what an unkind hour

Is guilty of this lamentable chance?

The lady stirs.




[Juliet wakes and stirs.]




JULIET.

O comfortable Friar, where is my lord?

I do remember well where I should be,

And there I am. Where is my Romeo?




[Noise within.]




FRIAR LAWRENCE.

I hear some noise. Lady, come from that nest

Of death, contagion, and unnatural sleep.

A greater power than we can contradict

Hath thwarted our intents. Come, come away.

Thy husband in thy bosom there lies dead;

And Paris too. Come, I’ll dispose of thee

Among a sisterhood of holy nuns.

Stay not to question, for the watch is coming.

Come, go, good Juliet. I dare no longer stay.





JULIET.

Go, get thee hence, for I will not away.




[Exit Friar Lawrence.]




What’s here? A cup clos’d in my true love’s hand?

Poison, I see, hath been his timeless end.

O churl. Drink all, and left no friendly drop

To help me after? I will kiss thy lips.

Haply some poison yet doth hang on them,

To make me die with a restorative.




[Kisses him.]




Thy lips are warm!




FIRST WATCH.

[Within.] Lead, boy. Which way?




JULIET.

Yea, noise? Then I’ll be brief. O happy dagger.




[Snatching Romeo’s
dagger.]




This is thy sheath. [stabs herself] There rest, and let me die.



[Falls on Romeo’s
body and dies.]



Enter Watch with the
Page of Paris.




PAGE.

This is the place. There, where the torch doth burn.





FIRST WATCH.

The ground is bloody. Search about the churchyard.

Go, some of you, whoe’er you find attach.




[Exeunt some of the Watch.]




Pitiful sight! Here lies the County slain,

And Juliet bleeding, warm, and newly dead,

Who here hath lain this two days buried.

Go tell the Prince; run to the Capulets.

Raise up the Montagues, some others search.




[Exeunt others of the Watch.]




We see the ground whereon these woes do lie,

But the true ground of all these piteous woes

We cannot without circumstance descry.




Re-enter some of the Watch with
Balthasar.




SECOND WATCH.

Here’s Romeo’s man. We found him in the churchyard.




FIRST WATCH.

Hold him in safety till the Prince come hither.



Re-enter others of the Watch with
Friar Lawrence.




THIRD WATCH.
Here is a Friar that trembles, sighs, and weeps.

We took this mattock and this spade from him

As he was coming from this churchyard side.





FIRST WATCH.

A great suspicion. Stay the Friar too.



Enter the Prince and
Attendants.




PRINCE.

What misadventure is so early up,

That calls our person from our morning’s rest?




Enter Capulet, Lady Capulet
and others.




CAPULET.

What should it be that they so shriek abroad?





LADY CAPULET.

O the people in the street cry Romeo,

Some Juliet, and some Paris, and all run

With open outcry toward our monument.





PRINCE.

What fear is this which startles in our ears?





FIRST WATCH.

Sovereign, here lies the County Paris slain,

And Romeo dead, and Juliet, dead before,

Warm and new kill’d.





PRINCE.

Search, seek, and know how this foul murder comes.





FIRST WATCH.

Here is a Friar, and slaughter’d Romeo’s man,

With instruments upon them fit to open

These dead men’s tombs.





CAPULET.

O heaven! O wife, look how our daughter bleeds!

This dagger hath mista’en, for lo, his house

Is empty on the back of Montague,

And it mis-sheathed in my daughter’s bosom.





LADY CAPULET.

O me! This sight of death is as a bell

That warns my old age to a sepulchre.




Enter Montague and others.




PRINCE.

Come, Montague, for thou art early up,

To see thy son and heir more early down.





MONTAGUE.

Alas, my liege, my wife is dead tonight.

Grief of my son’s exile hath stopp’d her breath.

What further woe conspires against mine age?





PRINCE.

Look, and thou shalt see.





MONTAGUE.

O thou untaught! What manners is in this,

To press before thy father to a grave?





PRINCE.

Seal up the mouth of outrage for a while,

Till we can clear these ambiguities,

And know their spring, their head, their true descent,

And then will I be general of your woes,

And lead you even to death. Meantime forbear,

And let mischance be slave to patience.

Bring forth the parties of suspicion.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

I am the greatest, able to do least,

Yet most suspected, as the time and place

Doth make against me, of this direful murder.

And here I stand, both to impeach and purge

Myself condemned and myself excus’d.





PRINCE.

Then say at once what thou dost know in this.





FRIAR LAWRENCE.

I will be brief, for my short date of breath

Is not so long as is a tedious tale.

Romeo, there dead, was husband to that Juliet,

And she, there dead, that Romeo’s faithful wife.

I married them; and their stol’n marriage day

Was Tybalt’s doomsday, whose untimely death

Banish’d the new-made bridegroom from this city;

For whom, and not for Tybalt, Juliet pin’d.

You, to remove that siege of grief from her,

Betroth’d, and would have married her perforce

To County Paris. Then comes she to me,

And with wild looks, bid me devise some means

To rid her from this second marriage,

Or in my cell there would she kill herself.

Then gave I her, so tutored by my art,

A sleeping potion, which so took effect

As I intended, for it wrought on her

The form of death. Meantime I writ to Romeo

That he should hither come as this dire night

To help to take her from her borrow’d grave,

Being the time the potion’s force should cease.

But he which bore my letter, Friar John,

Was stay’d by accident; and yesternight

Return’d my letter back. Then all alone

At the prefixed hour of her waking

Came I to take her from her kindred’s vault,

Meaning to keep her closely at my cell

Till I conveniently could send to Romeo.

But when I came, some minute ere the time

Of her awaking, here untimely lay

The noble Paris and true Romeo dead.

She wakes; and I entreated her come forth

And bear this work of heaven with patience.

But then a noise did scare me from the tomb;

And she, too desperate, would not go with me,

But, as it seems, did violence on herself.

All this I know; and to the marriage

Her Nurse is privy. And if ought in this

Miscarried by my fault, let my old life

Be sacrific’d, some hour before his time,

Unto the rigour of severest law.





PRINCE.

We still have known thee for a holy man.

Where’s Romeo’s man? What can he say to this?





BALTHASAR.

I brought my master news of Juliet’s death,

And then in post he came from Mantua

To this same place, to this same monument.

This letter he early bid me give his father,

And threaten’d me with death, going in the vault,

If I departed not, and left him there.





PRINCE.

Give me the letter, I will look on it.

Where is the County’s Page that rais’d the watch?

Sirrah, what made your master in this place?





PAGE.

He came with flowers to strew his lady’s grave,

And bid me stand aloof, and so I did.

Anon comes one with light to ope the tomb,

And by and by my master drew on him,

And then I ran away to call the watch.





PRINCE.

This letter doth make good the Friar’s words,

Their course of love, the tidings of her death.

And here he writes that he did buy a poison

Of a poor ’pothecary, and therewithal

Came to this vault to die, and lie with Juliet.

Where be these enemies? Capulet, Montague,

See what a scourge is laid upon your hate,

That heaven finds means to kill your joys with love!

And I, for winking at your discords too,

Have lost a brace of kinsmen. All are punish’d.





CAPULET.

O brother Montague, give me thy hand.

This is my daughter’s jointure, for no more

Can I demand.





MONTAGUE.

But I can give thee more,

For I will raise her statue in pure gold,

That whiles Verona by that name is known,

There shall no figure at such rate be set

As that of true and faithful Juliet.





CAPULET.

As rich shall Romeo’s by his lady’s lie,

Poor sacrifices of our enmity.





PRINCE.

A glooming peace this morning with it brings;

The sun for sorrow will not show his head.

Go hence, to have more talk of these sad things.

Some shall be pardon’d, and some punished,

For never was a story of more woe

Than this of Juliet and her Romeo.




[Exeunt.]

CETUP2026-014


Prepared for submission to JCAP


Interpretation of Nanohertz
Gravitational Waves via Cosmic
Strings in Post-BBN Matter and
Kination Domination


Raymond T. Co, Siu Cheung Lam, Taegyu Lee


Physics Department, Indiana University, Bloomington, IN 47405, USA


E-mail: rco@iu.edu, siuclam@iu.edu, taeglee@iu.edu


Abstract. Recent pulsar timing array (PTA) observations have reported evidence for a com-
mon low frequency signal consistent with a stochastic gravitational wave background. In
this work, we investigate the cosmic string interpretation of this signal in a non-standard
cosmological history between big bang nucleosynthesis and recombination, featuring an early
matter-dominated era followed by a kination phase. Previous studies have shown that kination
can significantly modify the cosmic-string-induced gravitational wave spectrum, generating
characteristic features in the nanohertz range and producing a shape consistent with current
PTA data. Building on this, we perform separate Bayesian analyses of the NANOGrav 15-year
and EPTA DR2 New+ datasets to quantify the extent to which such a cosmological history is
favored by the data. We find substantial evidence for a transient period of matter and kina-
tion domination over the standard cosmological history within a cosmic-string interpretation
of the gravitational-wave signal, with a Bayes factor 3.63+0.22


−0.21 from the NANOGrav 15-year
dataset, while leaving the inferred cosmic-string tension nearly unchanged. This cosmic-
string-dominated scenario is particularly relevant if the contribution from supermassive black
hole binaries (SMBHBs) is small, as hinted at by astrophysical population simulations. Com-
pared with a scenario where gravitational waves are produced purely by SMBHBs, cosmic-
string interpretations with and without an additional contribution from SMBHBs, across both
expansion histories and datasets, reveal no significant preference.

1 Introduction


The growing sensitivity of pulsar timing arrays (PTAs) has opened a new observational win-
dow onto the nanohertz gravitational wave (GW) regime. Recent results by the NANOGrav
15-year collaboration [1], the European Pulsar Timing Array (EPTA) [2], the Parkes Pulsar
Timing Array (PPTA) [3] and the Chinese Pulsar Timing Array (CPTA) [4] have reported
evidence for a stochastic GW background (SGWB) in the nanohertz regime. These develop-
ments have generated considerable interest since the PTA signal may provide a direct probe
not only of astrophysical sources such as supermassive black hole binaries (SMBHBs), but
also of relic processes that may have occurred in the early Universe. Possible early-Universe
cosmological sources relevant to the PTA band include cosmic strings (CS) [5, 6], first-order
phase transitions [7, 8], scalar-induced gravitational waves [9, 10], and gravitational waves
associated with non-standard cosmological histories such as kination [11, 12].


In the early Universe, cosmic strings can form during the symmetry breaking phase
transition via the Kibble mechanism [13, 14]. Depending on whether the underlying sponta-
neously broken symmetry is global or gauged, cosmic strings are broadly classified into global


– 1 –







and local strings. In this work, we focus on local cosmic strings. Such symmetry-breaking
patterns arise in a variety of particle physics models. A common example is the breaking
of an extra Abelian gauge symmetry such as U(1)B−L, which frequently appears in models
for neutrino mass generation based on the seesaw mechanism [15, 16]. Local strings may
also emerge in grand unified theories whose symmetry-breaking pattern contains an inter-
mediate U(1) symmetry, as in various SO(10) constructions [17, 18]. These scenarios make
local cosmic strings theoretically and phenomenologically well motivated. Once formed, they
follow a scaling law where the fraction of energy density in the network compared to the
background is fixed. Moreover, on scales much larger than their core width, local strings
are often well approximated by Nambu–Goto strings, so their network evolution leads to
predictive cosmological signatures, most notably an SGWB produced by oscillating cosmic
string loops [5, 19–21]. The resulting SGWB depends not only on string parameters such as
the tension, emission spectrum, and intercommutation probability, but also heavily on the
background expansion history of the Universe [11]. Therefore, the GW spectrum generated
by cosmic strings can reveal information not only about string microphysics but also about
the history of the cosmological evolution.


Among the simplest and well-motivated departures from the standard thermal history
is a kination-dominated (KD) era, during which the energy density of the Universe is domi-
nated by the kinetic energy of a scalar field [22, 23]. A particularly natural realization arises
in theories containing a complex scalar field with a global U(1) symmetry and a scalar poten-
tial whose non-zero vacuum expectation value spontaneously breaks the U(1) symmetry. In
addition, higher-dimensional operators explicitly break the symmetry and, for a large initial
radial field value, provide a kick in the angular direction that initiates field rotation in the
early Universe. While the radial mode is still displaced from its vacuum value, the scalar-field
energy can drive an early matter-dominated (MD) phase. As the radial mode thermalizes,
part of the energy stored in the complex scalar field is dissipated, while the approximately
conserved charge prevents the energy from being completely depleted. The motion then ap-
proaches circular rotation in field space. Once the radial mode settles to the vacuum, the
potential energy becomes negligible. In this regime, the energy density is dominated by rota-
tional kinetic energy, giving rise to a kination era. This kination era corresponds to a phase
with an equation of state parameter w ≃ 1, where the energy density redshifts as a−6 with a
the scale factor. Since this component redshifts faster than radiation, the kination phase is
temporary and the Universe eventually returns to the radiation-dominated (RD) epoch.


The PTA signal is also interesting from the viewpoint of non-standard expansion histo-
ries. Previous work has shown that if an early matter-dominated era is followed by kination,
with both transitions occurring between big bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) and recombina-
tion, the SGWB from cosmic strings can deviate substantially from the nearly flat spectrum
expected in the standard radiation-dominated cosmology [11]. In particular, this late matter–
kination sequence can modify the resulting spectral structure in the nanohertz range. Mo-
tivated by this possibility, in this work we perform a Bayesian analysis of the cosmic string
interpretation in the presence of such a late matter-to-kination domination history, and assess
whether it provides a better description of current PTA data than the standard cosmology.


The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Sec. 2, we review axion rotation
that can give rise to the kination cosmology considered in this work, characterized by an
early matter-dominated era followed by a kination-dominated era. In Sec. 3, we describe
GW production from local cosmic strings and detail the resulting spectrum in the presence
of the modified expansion history. In Sec. 4, we outline the Bayesian analysis framework and


– 2 –







describe the use of Bayes factors for model comparison. In Sec. 5, we present the constraints
imposed by BBN and cosmic microwave background (CMB) observations. The results of the
PTA analyses are presented and discussed in Sec. 6, followed by our conclusions in Sec. 7.


2 Axion rotation and kination


A simple and well-motivated origin of a KD era is provided by the rotation of a complex
scalar field that spontaneously breaks an approximate global U(1) symmetry [24]. We write
the complex scalar field as

with Affleck–Dine dynamics [34].


Once the explicit breaking term becomes negligible due to the redshift of the radial field

condensate, the corresponding charge density is


nθ ≡ iṖ ∗P − iṖP ∗ = S2θ̇, (2.5)


and it redshifts simply as 1/a3. Equivalently, in the absence of significant entropy production,
the yield


Yθ ≡
nθ


s
(2.6)


remains approximately constant, where s is the entropy density of the Universe. This con-
served charge is the key quantity that controls subsequent cosmological evolution. The im-
portant point is that the same rotating condensate can behave first as matter and later as
kination, depending on whether the rotation occurs at large radius or close to the vacuum
expectation value.


– 3 –







2.1 Matter-like evolution


The first stage arises while the radial field is still far from its vacuum value. In well-motivated
scenarios such as supersymmetric theories [35, 36], the radial potential can remain nearly
quadratic over a wide range of large field values despite the presence of a nonzero vacuum
expectation value. At this stage, we therefore approximate


V0(S) ≃
1


2
m2


SS
2, S ≫ fa. (2.7)


During this stage, the orbit is well described by rotational motion with a redshifting amplitude.
The angular velocity is determined by the radial equation of motion, which gives


θ̇2 ≃ V ′
0(S)


S
. (2.8)


For a quadratic potential, V ′
0(S) = m2


SS, and therefore


θ̇ ≃ mS . (2.9)


Since the conserved charge is nθ = S2θ̇ and nθ ∝ a−3, the orbit radius decreases as


S2 ∝ a−3. (2.10)


The energy stored in the rotating condensate is then dominated by the rotational and quadratic-
potential pieces, both of which scale in the same way. As a result, the energy density of the
rotation follows


ρrot ∝ a−3. (2.11)


Thus, while the field is rotating at large radius, the condensate behaves effectively as matter.
Importantly, this matter scaling is not caused by oscillations around the minimum in the
usual case, but by coherent rotation at the large field radius.


Although radial oscillations are inevitably triggered together with the initial kick, the
radial motion can later be depleted during the evolution. If the radial mode interacts with
the thermal bath, it can dissipate the energy density stored in the radial mode. This can
be done by perturbative decays of the radial mode or by scattering of the radial mode with
gauge bosons or with fermions in the thermal bath [24]. Thermalization becomes efficient
when Γth > H. However, if the interaction responsible for this dissipation does not break
the U(1) symmetry, it dissipates energy without affecting the associated charge, i.e., rotation
in the angular direction. Thermalization therefore does not remove the entire energy stored
in the complex scalar field. Rather, it removes the energy associated with radial motion,
rendering the rotational motion circular.


2.2 Kination-like evolution


As the Universe expands further, the charge density continues to dilute and the radius of the
orbit shrinks. Eventually the radial field approaches its vacuum value. At that point the
motion is no longer a rotation at a large radius, but rather a motion along the bottom of the
wine-bottle potential with nearly fixed radius. The conserved charge then implies


nθ = f2
a θ̇, (2.12)


– 4 –







so that the angular velocity decreases as θ̇ ∝ a−3. The complex field energy density is now
dominated by the angular kinetic term,


ρrot ≃
1


2
f2
a θ̇


2 =
n2
θ


2f2
a


∝ a−6, (2.13)


which is the scaling of kination and corresponds to an equation of state w = 1. We assume
that the axion is sufficiently light that axion rotation continues past TKR.


The essential picture is therefore very simple. At large radius, the energy per unit charge
is set by the radial mass scale, and the condensate behaves like matter. Once the radius reaches
fa, the same conserved charge is stored in pure angular motion, and the energy density begins
to redshift much faster, scaling as a−6.


2.3 Characteristic temperatures


In this work, both MD and KD are essential in producing the characteristic cosmic string
GW spectrum that is relevant for PTA observations. For this cosmological sequence to occur
between BBN and recombination, the radial mode should thermalize before the energy density
stored in the rotating condensate dominates over the radiation energy density. If this criterion
is satisfied, the Universe enters an early MD era and transitions to a KD era. As a result,
the cosmological history naturally proceeds from being RD to MD, to KD, and subsequently
to RD. This cosmology is illustrated schematically in Fig. 1. Importantly, neither transition
requires the dominant component to decay into radiation. In particular, unlike the usual early
matter domination that ends by reheating, the onset of kination need not be accompanied
by entropy production. The eventual transition from MD to KD and then to RD occurs
automatically through cosmic redshift.


It is useful to describe this history in terms of the approximately conserved yield Yθ =
nθ/s. Below, we denote by TRM, TMK, and TKR the temperatures at the time of the radiation-
to-matter, matter-to-kination, and kination-to-radiation transitions, respectively. During the
matter-dominated stage, the rotational energy density is of order

Figure 1. Schematic representation of the cosmological evolution of the different energy components
with scale factor a in the presence of a rotating field: RD → MD → KD → RD → MD. Radiation,
matter and scalar field energy densities are denoted by ρR, ρM and ρrot and are represented by orange,

– 6 –







The scenario described above is especially relevant for the cosmic string interpretation
studied in this work. The stochastic gravitational wave background emitted by a cosmic
string network is sensitive not only to the microscopic string parameters, but also to the
expansion history of the Universe. Consequently, a sequence such as RD → MD → KD → RD
can imprint characteristic breaks and changes of slope in the gravitational wave spectrum,
potentially shifting the relevant spectral features into the PTA band.


3 Cosmic string gravitational waves


3.1 Local cosmic strings and their gravitational wave background


Local cosmic strings can form when a U(1) gauge symmetry is spontaneously broken [13]. In
local strings, unlike global strings, the would-be Goldstone mode is eaten by the gauge field,
and the resulting defect is therefore a local string. Once they are formed, they quickly reach a
scaling regime consisting of two components: O(1) infinite (long) strings per Hubble volume,
whose radius of curvature is of order the Hubble length, H−1, together with a population of
sub-horizon-sized cosmic-string loops [37, 38]. Infinite strings intercommute with each other
and their energy density is continuously transferred to loops. These loops subsequently shrink
by emitting gravitational waves, and the present-day stochastic background is obtained by
summing the loop emission over the entire cosmic history. The gravitational wave spectrum
is calculated as


ΩGW(f) ≡ 1

the m-th harmonic,

encodes the loop number density and the cosmological redshift,

3.2 GW spectrum in standard cosmology


Before turning to non-standard expansion histories, it is useful to recall the benchmark spec-
tral behavior expected in the standard cosmological background. In this case, the observed
spectrum can be understood analytically by tracking the epoch in which loops are formed
and the epoch in which they predominantly emit GW.


An analytic estimate of the spectral shape is obtained by considering loops formed in
an era with an equation of state parameter w1 and emitting GW in an era with w2. For the
fundamental mode with m = 1, one finds [42]


Ω
(1)
GW(f) ∝ fλ, (3.9)


with


λ =




where tΓ denotes the characteristic time at which GW emission becomes important for the
loop evolution and p = −3+2/(1+w1)+4/(3(1+w2)). The parameter p characterizes which
emission times dominate the integral for Cm. For p < 0, the dominant contribution comes
from loops emitting near tΓ, whereas for p ≥ 0 it comes from the latest possible emission time
in the corresponding cosmological era. In the standard cosmological history, this reproduces
a distinct power law spectrum

so the total spectrum after summing over all m can be modified relative to the m = 1 estimate,
especially around spectral breaks. These standard-cosmology results serve as the reference
spectrum against which non-standard expansion histories can be compared.


3.3 GW spectrum modified by matter and kination eras


The important point for the present work is that cosmic strings are long lasting sources of
GW. Because of this, the observed frequency today carries information about both the epoch
when the relevant loops were formed and the epoch when they emit GW. Any non-standard
expansion can leave an imprint on the string induced SGWB, e.g., Refs. [43–46].


For sufficiently long MD and KD eras, the string spectrum is modified into a triangular
shape. If we neglect the sum over higher harmonics, one finds the following asymptotic
behavior


ΩGW(f) ∝



f0, f ≲ fKR,


f+1, fKR ≲ f ≲ fMK,


f−1, fMK ≲ f ≲ fRM,


f0, f ≳ fRM,


(3.13)


where fKR, fMK, and fRM denote the characteristic frequencies associated with the transitions
at TKR, TMK, and TRM, respectively. The origin of these slopes follows directly from Eq. (3.10).
The segment fKR ≲ f ≲ fMK probes loops associated with the kination era. Since kination
is a stiff phase (w = 1), the background energy density redshifts faster than radiation, so
the GW contribution from earlier times is weighted more strongly toward higher observed
frequencies, which leads to ΩGW ∝ f+1. The segment fMK ≲ f ≲ fRM instead probes loops
associated with the preceding MD. In that epoch, the expansion history shifts the emission
frequency relation in the opposite direction, leading to a negative spectral tilt towards higher
frequencies, which leads to ΩGW ∝ f−1. As a result, the standard broad plateau is converted
into a peaked structure around the matter-to-kination transition.


For realistic local strings, however, the observed spectrum is the sum over all harmonics.
For cusp-dominated loops, this leaves the kination slope essentially unchanged, while the high
frequency tail associated with the preceding matter era is substantially flattened. In that case,
Eq. (3.13) is replaced more accurately by


ΩGW(f) ∝



f0, f ≲ fKR,


f+1, fKR ≲ f ≲ fMK,


f−1/3, fMK ≲ f ≲ fRM,


f0, f ≳ fRM,


(3.14)


where the f−1/3 is due to cusp-dominated emission after summing over higher modes [41].
Hence, in the axion rotation cosmology discussed in Sec. 2, the local string SGWB


acquires a characteristic triangular bump or peaked broken power law. Its location is deter-
mined primarily by TMK through fMK, while its width is controlled by the duration of the
non-standard era, namely by the hierarchy between TRM and TKR. This feature is especially
important for the present work. Because the cosmic string signal is intrinsically broad, a

4 Bayesian analysis framework


In this section, we discuss the main components of statistical analysis within the Bayesian
framework, establishing a systematic approach for evaluating model evidence and, conse-
quently, the Bayes factor for model selection. For a more comprehensive review of Bayesian
analysis and model selection, we refer the reader to the literature, e.g., Refs. [47, 48].


For a model M and data D, the posterior is obtained from the Bayes’ theorem:


P(θ|D,M) =
π(θ|M)L(D|θ,M)


Z(D|M)
, (4.1)


where


• π(θ|M) is the prior of the parameter θ assuming model M , encoding all prior informa-
tion before observing the data D.


• L(D|θ,M) denotes the likelihood of data D given M and θ.


• Z(D|M) is the Bayesian evidence, which constitutes a crucial quantity for model com-
parisons. This can be obtained by integrating the likelihood over the parameter space
weighted by the prior


Z(D|M) =


∫
L(D|θ,M)π(θ|M)dθ. (4.2)


The Bayes factor Bij is used to compare two competing models, Mi, Mj with the dataset
D, within the Bayesian framework and is defined as the ratio of the respective Bayesian
evidence calculated from Eq. (4.2),


BD
ij =


Z(D|Mi)


Z(D|Mj)
. (4.3)


If BD
ij > 1, there is evidence in favor of model Mi over model Mj ; if BD


ij < 1, then Mj is
preferred over Mi. In this work, we quote the log Bayes factor, lnBD

and use Jeffreys’ scale [49, 50] to interpret the relative performance of any two competing
models Mi and Mj . The linear and log Jeffreys’ scales are summarized in Table 1. We explore
the parameter space for each model and the Bayesian evidence using UltraNest [51]. The
marginalized posteriors presented in this work are derived from the nested samples using
kernel density estimations implemented in the GetDist package [52].


Bayesian evidence naturally incorporates the principle of Occam’s razor, penalizing mod-
els with unnecessary complexity. Consequently, one can extract information about "goodness-
of-fit" and the model complexity from Bayes evidence. This can be achieved by decomposing
log evidence into two terms:

5 Analyses


In this section, we describe the setup adopted for the Bayesian analysis. Our main goal is to
determine how well the cosmic string induced SGWB can fit PTA data in the presence of an
intermediate non-standard cosmological era. To address this question, we consider a modified
expansion history between BBN and recombination, following the sequence


RD → MD → KD → RD.


As discussed in Sec. 3, such a non-standard history deforms the broad cosmic string spectrum
into a broken power-law shape with a characteristic bump. If the transition scales are between
BBN and recombination, this feature can be shifted into the nanohertz frequency range probed
by PTA experiments, where it may provide a better fit to the observed signal than the
prediction from standard cosmology. This motivates the study of not only the PTA fit, but
also whether the corresponding non-standard era remains consistent with BBN and the CMB.


In realistic UV complete models [24], the transitions between these epochs are smooth.
In the present analysis, however, we adopt a simplified description in which the background
evolution is treated piecewise with instantaneous transitions in the scaling of the scalar field’s
energy density with respect to the scale factor. This approximation captures the leading


– 12 –







impact of the non-standard era on the cosmic string gravitational wave spectrum while sim-
plifying the numerical calculation for the Bayesian analysis. The expansion history is therefore
specified by the characteristic transition temperatures TRM and TKR introduced in the previ-
ous sections with TMK automatically determined by Eq. (2.19). These parameters determine
the duration and timing of the MD and KD, and hence control the corresponding deformation
of the cosmic string spectrum relevant for PTA observations. Consequently, we compute the
time evolution of the scale factor a(t) from the first Friedmann equation

H0 = 68.6 km s−1 Mpc−1. These values are obtained from the Bayesian inference which will
be described in Sec. 5.1.2. The multiplicative factor

tion and depends upon the relativistic degrees of freedom for which we use the tabular data
computed in Ref. [56]. The case of Ωk,θ = Ωm,θ = 0 in Eq. (5.1) corresponds to ΛCDM. In the
following sections, we examine the constraints from BBN and the CMB on the non-standard
cosmology parameters TRM and TKR in Sec. 5.1, followed by PTA analysis in Sec. 5.2.


5.1 Constraints


Our inference proceeds in three stages. The non-standard expansion history is parametrized
by the radiation-to-matter and kination-to-radiation transition temperatures, TRM and TKR,
with TMK determined by Eq. (2.19). First, we use BBN light-element abundances to constrain
TRM and construct its marginalized posterior distribution. We then fix TRM to the 2σ BBN
upper bound, together with the corresponding helium abundance, and use CMB and baryon
acoustic oscillations (BAO) observations to constrain TKR. The prior range for TKR is chosen
to ensure that the Universe returns to radiation domination before recombination while main-
taining the required ordering TKR ≤ TRM. As shown below, the inferred TKR distribution is
largely insensitive to variations of TRM within its BBN-allowed range, motivating the separate
BBN and CMB+BAO fits. Finally, kernel-density estimates of the marginalized BBN and
CMB+BAO posteriors are used as informative priors for TRM and TKR, respectively, in the
PTA analysis, where both transition temperatures are varied together with the cosmic-string
and astrophysical parameters. Thus, BBN and CMB+BAO first determine the allowed non-
standard expansion histories, while the NANOGrav and EPTA data subsequently test their
imprint on the gravitational-wave spectrum.

5.1.1 BBN


An early matter domination will enhance the expansion rate. For a transient matter domina-
tion at a temperature of the order O(keV), observations of primordial helium and deuterium
abundances provide a sensitive probe to constrain TRM as faster expansion shortens the dura-
tion of the nuclear-burning phase, thereby altering the primordial light-element abundances.


To study the effect of a brief departure from standard cosmology on light-element abun-
dances, we modify the publicly available BBN solver PRyMordial [61] to include the axion


– 14 –







energy density in the thermal background. We adopt a Gaussian prior N (η̄10, σ
2
η10) on the


baryon-to-photon ratio, parametrized as η10 = 1010ηB, where η̄10 and ση10 are converted from
Planck-inferred Ωbh


2 [62]. We adopt the Particle Data Group [63] average neutron lifetime
τn = 878.3±0.4 s, derived from eight measurements made with ultracold neutrons [64–71] and
impose a Gaussian prior τn ∼ N (878.3, 0.42). Through smallnet=True flag in the PRyMor-
dial configuration file, we marginalize over twelve key nuclear reaction rates from NACRE II
compilation [72], Γ⃗ = (Γ1,Γ2, ...,Γ12), that are necessary to accurately predict the primordial
helium and deuterium abundances. We model each nuclear rate as log-normal distributed:


log Γi = log⟨Γi⟩+ βi log (∆Γi) , (5.3)


where βi is the nuisance parameter governing the variation of i-th nuclear reaction rate sam-
pled from a normal distribution, ⟨Γi⟩ denotes the median rate, and ∆Γi is the corresponding
uncertainty factor. We define the log likelihood function

We use emcee [73], an affine-invariant ensemble sampler [74], to derive the posterior dis-


tributions for the 15-dimensional parameter space (TRM, η10, τn, β⃗) with TKR fixed to 150 eV.
We run Nwalker = 128 for 50000 iterations each. The first 35% of each chain is discarded
as burn-in. We assess convergence by first finding the maximum integrated autocorrelation
time τmax

ESS = Nwalker × Ns/τ

in. This yields an ESS ≈ 1.8 × 104. The one- and two-dimensional marginalized posterior
distributions are shown in Fig. 3. We find the 68% and 95% C.L. upper bounds on TRM as


TRM ≤ 6.45 keV, (5.5)
TRM ≤ 12.6 keV. (5.6)


In the joint CMB+BAO analysis presented in Sec. 5.1.2, when we impose the tempera-
ture ordering TKR ≤ TRM, we adopt the 2σ value of TRM as the upper bound on TKR. The
banana-shape in the TRM-Yp posterior reveals a strong correlation between the two parame-
ters. This results in a stringent constraint on TRM from BBN. To obtain a representative Yp
at the 2σ(1σ) upper bound of TRM, we select samples within a finite interval centered on the
2σ(1σ) upper bound of TRM and take the corresponding median of Yp. This procedure yields
Yp ∼ 0.2487(0.2478), which we adopt as an input to the Boltzmann solver CLASS. The TRM


samples provide an informative prior for the GW background analysis in Sec. 5.2.


5.1.2 CMB


The presence of a kination epoch can affect the CMB anisotropies and CMB lensing power
spectrum. As discussed in Ref. [11], the modified expansion rate can alter the evolution of
cosmological perturbations and reduce the comoving sound horizon rs. This modifies the
acoustic scale and can also alter the matter power spectrum, potentially leaving observable


– 15 –







signatures in structure-growth and distance-scale probes such as CMB lensing and BAO. To
quantify these effects and constrain the non-standard cosmology parameters, we perform a
joint likelihood analysis of the CMB and BAO data using CLASS [75], modified to incorporate
the non-standard cosmological history described earlier, together with the Cobaya sampler [76,
77]. We take six ΛCDM parameters and one non-standard cosmology parameter TKR,


{ωb, ωc, H0, ln(10
10As), ns, τreio, TKR}, (5.7)


as free parameters while fixing TRM to a value near the upper bound allowed by BBN at the
2σ level in Eq. (5.6), by which we require TKR to be bounded from above. The parameter
priors used in the CMB analysis are summarized in Table 2. Since TRM is strongly correlated
with the primordial helium abundance Yp (Fig. 3), we determine the median value of Yp for
each fixed TRM. The resulting pair is (TRM, Yp) = (12.6 keV, 0.2487), which is adopted as
an input for the background cosmology in CLASS. We verify that the posterior distribution
of TKR is largely insensitive to variations in TRM. We therefore perform separate fits to the
BBN and CMB data.


Parameter Prior
ωb U(0.020, 0.025)
ωc U(0.095, 0.145)
H0 U(60, 75)
ln(1010As) U(2.5, 3.9)
ns U(0.86, 1.06)
τreio U(0.01, 0.15)
log10(TKR/eV) U(0, 4.10)


Table 2. Prior used in the CMB analysis. Here U denotes a uniform distribution.


For the CMB likelihoods, we use the Planck 2018 temperature and polarization power
spectra, including the low-ℓ TT, EE, high-ℓ TTTEEE likelihoods [62], and CMB lensing [78].
For the BAO likelihood, we include BAO measurements from DESI DR2 [79]. The posterior is
sampled using MCMC implemented in Cobaya, and convergence is declared once the Gelman–
Rubin criterion [80] satisfies |R− 1| < 0.005. The posterior distribution for the cosmological
parameters is shown in Fig. 4.


5.2 PTAs and analyses setup


A passing gravitational wave perturbs the spacetime metric along the line of sight between
millisecond pulsars and Earth, which induces a time-dependent perturbation in pulse time-of-
arrival measurements. This effect can be parametrized by a one-sided timing-residual power
spectral density (PSD) S(fi) = Φ(fi)Tobs at each frequency bin fi = i/Tobs, where Tobs is
the observation baseline and i is the harmonic number. The fractional GW energy density is
related to the PSD via the relation


ΩGW(f)h2 =

sity distributions inferred from the Hellings–Downs (HD) correlated free-spectrum analyses

Baseline model: Supermassive black hole binaries— As our baseline astrophysical
interpretation of the nanohertz gravitational wave background, we consider a population of
SMBHBs. SMBHBs are one of the most popular astrophysical sources of an SGWB in the
nanohertz frequency regime. This is mainly because SMBHBs are expected to be abundant
in the Universe, and because the GWs emitted during their inspiral can easily fall within the
frequency band probed by pulsar timing arrays.


The GW spectrum from SMBHBs can be parametrized as [86]


ΩGWh2 =
2π2f2


yrA
2
SMBHB


3H2
100


(
f


fyr


)5−γSMBHB


, (5.9)


where H100 = 100 km s−1Mpc−1, fyr = 1/yr, ASMBHB is the GW amplitude from the SMBHB
population, and γSMBHB is the power law spectral index. The amplitude is primarily deter-
mined by the masses of the SMBHBs and by the abundance of SMBHBs contributing to the
population. On the other hand, the spectral index encodes information about how SMBHBs
evolve before and during the GW driven inspiral phase.


Currently, both the amplitude and the spectral index remain weakly constrained by
observations and numerical simulations. The current constraint is obtained from astrophys-
ical simulations using the holodeck package [87], which implements semi-analytic modeling
of SMBHB population. For our baseline hypothesis H0, we therefore adopt a simulation-
informed prior on the parameters (log10ASMBHB, γSMBHB). Specifically, they follow a two-
dimensional Gaussian distribution whose mean and covariance matrix are obtained from fit-
ting to numerical simulations [88],

Alternative hypotheses: cosmic strings— We consider four alternative hypotheses:
cosmic-string models within either ΛCDM or the modified cosmology (kin) described in
Sec. 3.3, with SMBHB contributions (CS+SMBHBs, kin-CS+SMBHBs) or without them
(CS, kin-CS). The descriptions and parameters of each model are summarized in Table 3. For
all alternative models, the cosmic-string GW spectrum in Eq. (3.1) is truncated to the first 104


modes1 and we assume that the GW power spectrum is dominated by cusps (q = 4/3). For
the kination parameters, we use the posterior samples of log10(TRM/eV) and log10(TKR/eV)
from the independent BBN and CMB analyses directly as informative priors. We implement
prior transformations that map unit cube variables to log10 TRM and log10 TKR in UltraNest
according to the empirical posterior distribution encoded in the corresponding BBN and
CMB chains. We adopt a stopping criterion of frac_remain=0.01 and require a minimum of
nlive=400 live points and an effective sample size of at least 1000 to declare convergence.


1Higher harmonics contribute to higher frequency GW as can be seen from Eq. (3.6). We truncate the
harmonic sum at 104 beyond which we find no appreciable change in the predicted GW spectrum in the
nanohertz regime.

6 Results


We now present the results of our Bayesian analyses of the NG15 and EPTA DR2 New+
datasets. To isolate the impact of the modified expansion history associated with kination,
we first compare the cosmic-string models in ΛCDM and kination cosmologies, taking the
SMBHB-only model as the common baseline H0. We then perform the same comparison for
models that include an additional SMBHB contribution. Finally, we directly compare the
ΛCDM and kination scenarios within the cosmic-string framework, both with and without an
additional SMBHB contribution.


The inferred parameter constraints for all models are summarized in Tables 4 and 5
for the NG15 and EPTA DR2 New+ datasets, respectively. The corresponding Bayes factors
relative to the baseline hypothesis H0 are reported in Table 6. The direct comparisons between
kination and ΛCDM within the cosmic-string framework with and without SMBHBs are
summarized in Table 7. All quoted parameter constraints correspond to 68% credible intervals.

alternative hypothesis favored over the SMBHB-only baseline H0, owing to its more favorable

We next assess the effect of kination by directly comparing the kination hypothesis
with its ΛCDM counterpart within the cosmic-string framework. For the cosmic-string-only
model, this corresponds to comparing H3 with H1, while for models including SMBHBs, we
compare H4 with H2. The corresponding log Bayes factors are summarized in Table 7. The
direct comparison between H3 and H1 in NG15 dataset indicates substantial support for
kination over ΛCDM, yielding lnBNG15


31 = 1.29 ± 0.06, or equivalently BNG15
31 = 3.63+0.22


−0.21.
This comparison is particularly relevant if the SMBHB contribution to the SGWB is small,
as hinted at by astrophysical population simulations, in which case the PTA signal may be
predominantly sourced by cosmic strings. The role of kination cosmology is apparent; the
presence of a brief matter-kination epoch leads to a spectral enhancement over its counterpart
in ΛCDM through the modified expansion history, improving the fit sufficiently to overcome
the associated increase in the KL penalty and consequently increasing the log Bayes factor.
This improvement occurs because the GW spectrum depends on the expansion history of the
universe when the cosmic-string loops emit GWs. As discussed in Sec. 3.3, a brief period of
matter-kination epoch modifies the expansion history and hence the relation between loop
formation and emission times and the observed GW frequency, altering the spectral shape
relative to ΛCDM. As shown by Eq. (3.14), these effects produce a blue-tilted spectrum,
ΩGW ∝ f , for loops associated with the kination era, enhancing the higher-frequency part of
the spectrum relative to ΛCDM, despite the nearly unchanged constraints on cosmic-string
tension. This enhancement allows the model to better accommodate the blue-tilted ΩGWh2


spectrum inferred from the NG15 data. This preference, however, is reduced once contri-
butions from SMBHBs are included. In contrast, EPTA shows little ability to distinguish
between the two cosmologies, with kination yielding a slightly positive log Bayes factor.


6.2 Gravitational-wave spectra


The SGWB spectra for all hypotheses and PTA dataset considered in this work are presented
in this section. For each hypothesis, parameter samples are redrawn from the posterior and
mapped onto the corresponding GW energy-density spectrum, ΩGWh2. The median spectrum
and 68% credible interval are determined from the resulting ensemble of spectral realizations
at each frequency. For comparison, we show the SMBHB contributions to the SGWB denoted
by a black dashed line in each plot throughout this section, adopting the fiducial values
log10ASMBHB = −15.6 and γSMBHB = 4.7, obtained from astrophysical simulations [88].

Figure 9. The median gravitational wave spectra induced by the four alternative hypotheses for the
NG15 dataset. The blue violins represent the HD-correlated free spectra. (a) Cosmic-string spectra
in ΛCDM (CS, blue) and kination (kin-CS, red) cosmologies. (b) The corresponding spectra super-
imposed with an additional SMBHB contribution (CS+SMBHBs, green; kin-CS+SMBHBs, orange).
The shaded bands indicate the 68% credible intervals. The black dashed lines represent the GW
signal from SMBHBs with log10 ASMBHB = −15.6 and γSMBHB = 4.7, which are the mean values from
astrophysical simulations [88].


The SGWB spectra of the alternative models fitted to the NG15 dataset are shown in
Fig. 9. The cosmic-string-only models in the two cosmologies are shown in Fig. 9(a). The
kination scenario produces a modest enhancement toward the high-frequency end compared
to ΛCDM although the cosmic-string tension posteriors remain largely consistent between H1


and H3 (Fig. 5). Fig. 9(b) reveals that once contributions from SMBHBs are included in the
two scenarios, the spectra become more similar, with the median spectra and the credible
intervals overlapping significantly over the frequencies probed by NG15. This indicates that
the additional degrees of freedom from SMBHBs can effectively absorb the spectral differences
between the two cosmological frameworks and therefore the modifications associated with a
non-standard cosmological expansion history can only be weakly resolved by NG15.


Figure 10 shows the SGWB spectra for the EPTA DR2 New+ dataset. Similar to
the NG15 results, the SGWB spectra for the cosmic-string-only model in the ΛCDM and
kination scenarios are broadly consistent across the frequency range probed by EPTA, with
substantial overlap between their 68% credible intervals as shown in figure 10(a). While the
inferred string tensions remain consistent between the two cosmologies, the presence of a
non-standard cosmology leads to a slight enhancement of the reconstructed spectrum toward
the high-frequency end. When an additional SMBHB contribution is included, however, the
spectral differences between the two cosmological scenarios become less pronounced, with the
reconstructed spectra remaining largely overlapping across the EPTA frequency range.

7 Conclusion and outlook


Our primary objective here is to investigate how a non-standard expansion history featuring
an early matter-dominated era followed by a kination phase between BBN and recombination
can leave characteristic imprints on the SGWB sourced by cosmic strings in the nanohertz
regime probed by NG15 and EPTA DR2 New+ pulsar timing array datasets. We perform
a comprehensive Bayesian analysis and model comparison for cosmic strings in both ΛCDM
and kination cosmologies, with and without additional SMBHB contributions.


Our analysis finds no strong evidence favoring any of the alternative hypotheses over
the SMBHB-only baseline H0, in either dataset. The underperformance of the cosmic-string
model in describing NG15 arises from the preference for a blue-tilted ΩGWh2 spectrum. A blue
tilt can be accommodated within the cosmic-string framework by moving toward smaller string
tensions, but the resulting reduction in the overall amplitude makes it difficult to account for
the large amplitude inferred by the dataset. Consequently, the cosmic-string spectrum in
ΛCDM is either too weak or too flat to simultaneously account for the inferred amplitude
and spectral tilt. The kination scenario considered in this work enhances the SGWB spectrum
toward the high-frequency end, resulting in a more pronounced blue tilt and thereby providing
a better fit compared to the counterpart in ΛCDM. In contrast to NG15, the EPTA DR2
New+ data slightly favor a flatter SGWB spectrum ΩGWh2, which is readily accommodated
by larger cosmic-string tension. This is consistent with the higher median values of log10Gµ


– 27 –







inferred from EPTA relative to NG15 across four alternative models. Nonetheless, within
the cosmic string framework, with or without contributions from SMBHBs, both datasets
mildly favor kination over ΛCDM, with the strength of preference ranging from negligibly
small in EPTA to substantial for the cosmic-string-only model in NG15. In spite of the
differences in evidence, the inferred cosmic-string-tension posteriors remain largely consistent
between the two cosmologies. The ΩGWh2 credible intervals also show substantial overlap
across the frequency range probed by NG15 and EPTA DR2 New+. We also show that
the current EPTA DR2 New+ dataset does not provide sufficient sensitivity to distinguish
the kination and ΛCDM expansion histories within the cosmic-string framework. The NG15
dataset shows a stronger preference for kination with the cosmic-string-only model providing
substantial evidence lnBNG15


31 = 1.29 ± 0.06. This preference diminishes once SMBHBs are
included.


A joint analysis of the NG15 and EPTA DR2 NEW+ datasets could, in principle, im-
prove the statistical sensitivity and provide tighter constraints on model parameters and
model evidences. However, the two PTA datasets contain overlapping pulsars; combining the
two likelihoods will double count information associated with the common pulsars, potentially
biasing the model constraints and model evidences. A statistically consistent joint analysis
will therefore require dedicated statistical treatments of the correlations between overlapping
datasets. The forthcoming International Pulsar Timing Array Third Data Release (IPTA
DR3) [89] will combine observations from multiple PTA collaborations and will provide a
more comprehensive joint dataset for characterizing the SGWB. This is particularly relevant
for the present work since NG15 and EPTA DR2 New+ exhibit different spectral preferences
and yield somewhat different statistical supports for kination cosmology. A consistent joint
analysis will clarify the nature of the SGWB and its implication for the early expansion
history, which we leave to future work.
